L1358: ## 13.5.1.2 Immunogenicity assays: bioanalytical methods for testing anti-drug antibodies (ADA) and neutralizing ADA
L1359: The applicant used electrochemiluminescence (ECL)-based bridging immunoassays for assessment of anti-drug antibodies (ADA) in tildrakizumab clinical trials. In brief, immunogenicity samples were diluted and incubated with biotinylated tildrakizumab (capture reagent) and SULFO-TAG™-labeled tildrakizumab (detection reagent), and aliquots of the mixture were then pipetted onto streptavidin-coated Meso Scale Discovery® plate. After washing and removal of unbound molecules, the ECL response from the bound SULFO-TAG™-labeled tildrakizumab was detected.
L1360: Confirmed ADA positive samples were tested for neutralizing ADA (NAb). The NAb assay was a competitive ligand binding assay using biotinylated human IL-23 as capture reagent and SULFO-TAG™-labeled human IL-23 as detection reagent. There were three generations of ADA and NAb immunoassays for immunogenicity assessment during the clinical development program. The three generations used the same ECL bridging design but differed in assay reagents (e.g., positive antibody controls) and assay characterization.
L1361: Table 59 and Table 60 provide the sensitivity and drug tolerance level of the ADA and NAb immunogenicity assays. Below is a summary of immunogenicity assay information relevant to Phase 3 immunogenicity data assessment.
L1362: ## Sensitivity and drug tolerance of the immunogenicity assays used in Phase 3 trials
L1363: The ECL bridging assay had drug tolerance levels of 6 mcg/mL and &lt;1 mcg/mL of tildrakizumab in serum for detection of 500 ng/mL and 30 ng/mL ADA, respectively. In Phase 3 trials, the mean±SD steady state tildrakizumab trough concentrations at Week 16 were 2.25±1.88 mcg/mL and 1.22±0.939 for the 200 mg and 100 mg doses, respectively, in Study P010; and 2.99±2.16 mcg/mL and 1.47±1.12 for the 200 mg and 100 mg doses, respectively, in Study P011. The Phase 3 PK data suggest that drug concentrations in immunogenicity samples would interfere
L1364: the ECL bridging assay for detection of ADA &lt; 30 ng/mL and would not interfere ADA detection when ADA levels are ≥500 ng/mL.
L1365: The detection of NAb could be interfered with the presence of tildrakizumab in serum because the mean±SD steady state serum tildrakizumab concentrations were above the drug tolerance level of 99 ng/mL of the NAb assay.
L1366: Refer to the Product Quality Review for more information regarding the performance and validation of the immunogenicity assays.

L1373: ## 13.5.2. Clinical Pharmacology Studies
L1374: ## 13.5.2.1 Study P009: A clinical drug interaction study that evaluated the effect of tildrakizumab on PK of CYP450 substrates in subjects with psoriasis
L1375: ## BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - BLA 761067
L1376: ## ILUMYA (tildrakizumab) injection, for subcutaneous use
L1377: Study P009 was a randomized, fixed-sequence, two periods, parallel group, open-label, clinical drug interaction study that evaluated the effect of tildrakizumab on PK of CYP450 substrates in subjects with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis. Study P009 also explored the relative bioavailability of tildrakizumab delivered via prefilled syringe (PFS) and autoinjector (AI) presentations.
L1378: ## Study design and treatment:
L1379: Period 1: On Day 1 of Period 1, subjects received a single oral dose of a cocktail containing CYP450 probe substrates CYP3A4 (2 mg of midazolam), 2C19 (40 mg of omeprazole), CYP1A2 (200 mg of caffeine), CYP2C9 (10 mg of warfarin) and CYP2D6 (30 mg of dextromethorphan). There was at least 7-days washout period between Period 1 and Period 2.
L1380: Period 2: On Day 1 and Day 29 of Period 2, subjects received 200 mg dose of tildrakizumab via SC injection by PFS (n=10) or AI (n=10). On Day 57, subjects received a single dose of the CYP450 probe substrates cocktail.
L1381: Blood samples to evaluate the PK of the probe substrates and tildrakizumab were collected during the study.
L1382: ## Study population
L1383: Twenty subjects were randomized: 17 subjects completed the study and 3 subjects (1 in PFS arm and 2 in AI arm) discontinued.
L1384: The demographic data based on the 20 subjects are summarized below:
L1385: - Sex: 13 male (65%); 7 female (35%)
- Age (median): 39 years (range: 22-63 years)
- Race: 20 Caucasian (100%)
- Body weight (mean\pmSD): 84\pm12 kg
- Baseline psoriasis area and severity (PASI): median 17; range: 12-42
L1386: ## PK of tildrakizumab:
L1387: Tildrakizumab PK results following SC administrations of tildrakizumab 200 mg on Day 1 and on Day 29 via PFS or AI are summarized in Table 61. Note that the current study is not powered to demonstrate PK comparability between PFS and AI presentations and the applicant did not propose to register the AI presentation in the current BLA.
L1388: **Table 61: Summary of tildrakizumab PK parameters following a single SC dose administration of 200 mg tildrakizumab via PFS or AI (Study P009)**

L1393: ## Effect of tildrakizumab on PK of CYP450 substrates
L1394: The effect of tildrakizumab, 200 mg administered SC at Weeks 0 and 4, on the PK of caffeine (metabolized by CYP1A2), warfarin (metabolized by CYP2C9), omeprazole (metabolized by CYP2C19), dextromethorphan (metabolized by CYP2D6), and midazolam (metabolized by CYP3A4) were evaluated in 14 to 20 evaluable subjects with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis. Changes in AUC_{inf} of caffeine, warfarin, omeprazole, and midazolam were not considered clinically relevant. The AUC_{inf} of dextromethorphan was increased by 20%. Table 62 to Table 66 provides the statistical comparison of the plasma PK parameters of each CYP450 substrate in the probe cocktail: midazolam, S-warfarin, 5-hydroxyomeprazole/omeprazole, dextromethorphan, and caffeine, respectively.

L1413: randomized to receive 0.1 mg/kg (n=4), 0.5 mg/kg (n=4), 3 mg/kg (n=8), and 10 mg/kg (n=6) or placebo (n=7). The results of tildrakizumab PK parameters are summarized in Table 67.

L1422: ## 13.5.2.4 Study P06306: influence of race on single dose PK of tildrakizumab in healthy subjects
L1423: Study P06306 was an open-label, non-randomized, single-dose PK study in Japanese, Chinese and White healthy subjects. Study P06306 had 2 parts. In Part 1, 22 Japanese, 19 White and 12 Chinese subjects were assigned to one of 3 dose groups: 50 mg (8 Japanese, 7 White, and 4 Chinese), 200 mg (6 Japanese, 6 White, and 4 Chinese) and 400 mg (8 Japanese, 6 White, and 4 Chinese). In Part 2, 6 Japanese subjects received 10 mg/kg IV of tildrakizumab. The tildrakizumab PK parameters are summarized in Table 69. The PK results supported that:
L1424: - PK of tildrakizumab in Japanese and Caucasian healthy subjects appeared to be similar.
- Tildrakizumab showed dose proportional PK over the dose range from 50 mg to 400 mg following SC administration.

L1429: ## 13.5.2.5 Study P05382: A rising multiple dose study of tildrakizumab in subjects with psoriasis
L1430: Study P05382 was a 3-part, multiple-dose, randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind (within dose group) trial in subjects with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis.
L1431: In Part 1, subjects were randomized to active or placebo (3:1) within each dose cohort: 0.1 mg/kg (n=3), 0.5 mg/kg (n=3), 3 mg/kg (n=7), 10 mg/kg (n=6) or placebo (n=6). Subjects received treatment on Day 1, Day 56 and Day 84.
L1432: In Part 2, subjects were randomized to active or placebo (3:1) within each dose cohort: 3 mg/kg (n=15), 10 mg/kg (n=14) or placebo (n=11). Subjects received treatment on Day 1, Day 28 and Day 56.
L1433: In Part 3, subjects were randomized to receive 0.05 mg/kg (n=6), 0.1 mg/kg (n=3) or placebo (n=3) on Day 1, Day 56, and Day 84.
L1434: PK Results:
L1435: Tildrakizumab in general showed dose-proportional increases in exposure. The mean clearance values ranged from 1.57 to 2.50 mL/day/kg and the mean apparent volume of distribution (during the terminal phase) ranged from 58.3 to 91.6 mL/kg. The mean apparent half-life values ranged from 20.2 to 26.9 days.

L1441: ## 13.5.3. Immunogenicity
L1442: ## 13.5.3.1 Immunogenicity incidences
L1443: In the combined Phase 3 trials, P010 and P011, approximately 4.3% (26/611) and 3.6% (22/614) of subjects had treatment-emergent anti-drug antibodies (TE-ADA) to tildrakizumab following the initial 12 weeks treatment with the 100 mg or 200 mg dosage regimens, respectively. The TE-ADA incidences were 6.8% (25/369) and 8.8% (29/329) following treatment up to 64 weeks for the 100 mg or 200 mg dosage regimens, respectively. The incidences for neutralizing antibodies (NAb) among TE-ADA+ subjects were 40.0% (10/25) and 37.9% (11/29) for the 100 mg or 200 mg dosage regimens through Week 64, respectively. More detailed immunogenicity incidences based on different ADA categories and the definition of the ADA categories are summarized in Table 71 and Table 72.
L1444: **Table 71: Summary of immunogenicity incidences through Week 12 in Phase 3 trials (P010 and P011).**

L1448: Definitions of subjects ADA status are summarized below:
L1449: - **ADA negative (ADA-):** All pre-treatment and post-dose samples were negative and the drug concentration in the last post-dose sample was below the drug tolerance level for the ADA assay.
- **ADA inconclusive:** All pre-treatment and post-dose samples were negative and the drug concentration in the last post-dose sample was equal or above the drug tolerance level for the ADA assay.
- **Treatment-emergent ADA (TE-ADA):** TE-ADA include (1) subjects who had negative pre-treatment sample and at least one positive post-dose sample and (2) subjects who had both positive pre-treatment sample and positive post-dose sample and the titer increased by \ge 2-fold.TE-ADA positive NAb negative: TE-ADA positive but negative for NAb
- TE-ADA positive NAb positive: TE-ADA positive and positive for NAb
- **Non-treatment-emergent ADA (non-TE-ADA):** non-TE-ADA include (1) subjects who had positive pre-treatment sample and post-dose samples were negative; and (2) subjects who had positive pre-treatment and post-dose samples with a &lt; 2-fold increase in titer post-dose.Non-TE-ADA positive NAb negative: Non-TE-ADA positive and negative for NAb
- Non-TE-ADA positive NAb positive: Non-TE-ADA positive and positive for NAb
L1450: (Source of data: Tables 2.7.2:14-16, Summary of Clinical Pharmacology)

L1455: ## 13.4.3.2 Impact of immunogenicity on pharmacokinetics
L1456: The PK results showed that subjects who developed TE-ADA-NAb were associated with lower tildrakizumab concentrations compared with ADA negative subjects. The effect of ADA on tildrakizumab concentrations was evaluated in subjects receiving continuous 100 mg or 200 mg doses through 52-64 weeks in Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials (Figure 18).
L1457: Figure 18: Mean tildrakizumab concentration-time profiles by subject ADA status in Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials.
L1458: Definitions of subjects ADA status are described in Table 71
L1459: (*Source of data: Figure 13 and Figure 15, Immunogenicity Summary Report 04fcnm.pdf*)
L1460: ## 13.4.3.3 Impact of immunogenicity on efficacy
L1461: The pooled efficacy results from Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials showed that subjects who developed TE-ADA-NAb had reduced response rate compared with ADA negative subjects. For example, the proportions of subjects who achieved PASI 75 response were 50% and 25% for the 100 mg and 200 mg dosage regimens in TE-ADA-NAb subjects, respectively, in comparison to 63.9% and 63.5% in ADA negative subjects (Table 73). Similar negative impact of TE-ADA-NAb on efficacy was also observed when using PASI 90, PASI 100 and PGA as efficacy endpoints (data not shown).
L1462: Table 73: Effect of ADA on PASI 75 response rate at Week 12 in pooled Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials (P010 and P011).

L1473: ## 13.5.4. Population PK analysis
L1474: The applicant submitted a population PK report entitled “*Population Pharmacokinetic Analysis of MK-3222 in Healthy Subjects and Subjects with Psoriasis*” (*Report 04k8q6.pdf*) to characterize the PK of tildrakizumab after SC administration.
L1475: ## Objectives:
L1476: The objectives of the population PK analysis were to investigate:
L1477: ## BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - BLA 761067
L1478: ## ILUMYA (tildrakizumab) injection, for subcutaneous use
L1479: - The PK characteristics of tildrakizumab
- The relationship between covariates and exposure; e.g. psoriatic subject characteristics such as body weight, gender, age, race and ethnicity (Hispanic, Japanese).
- To explore the potential impact of the lyophilized formulation (Phase 1 and 2) versus the solution formulation in prefilled syringe (Phase 3) on exposure
L1480: ## **Data:**
L1481: The population PK model was developed based on data from three Phase 1 trials (P05776, P06306, and P009), one Phase 2 trial (P05495), and two Phase 3 trials (P010 and P011). Only active arms and SC treatment arms were included. The full PK analysis dataset consisted of 2106 subjects and 18491 observations in total, among which, 87 subjects were healthy and 2019 were subjects with psoriasis. The final PK datasets included 2098 subjects with 17321 observation records. Descriptive statistics of continuous covariates and categorical covariates are shown in Table 75 and Table 76, respectively.

L1489: ## Population PK Model Development
L1490: ## Base Model:
L1491: The selected model was a 1-compartment model (parameterized in terms of clearance (CL) and volume of distribution (V)) with first order absorption and elimination, and inter-individual variability on CL, V and absorption rate constant (KA). The residual error structure was combined proportional and additive. The difference between healthy subjects and subjects with psoriasis was partially explained by BW. Hence, BW was included as structural covariate on apparent total clearance (CL/F) and apparent volume of distribution (V/F) in the base model as well. The parameter estimates of the base model for tildrakizumab are summarized in Table 77. The goodness-of-fit plots for the base model are presented in Figure 19.
L1492: **Table 77: Parameter Estimates of the Base Model for Tildrakizumab**

L1497: ## **Final Model:**
L1498: Covariates in the final model included BW, age, serum albumin, race, ethnicity and gender on CL/F; BW, age and gender on V/F; subject population and formulation on F. Most covariate effects were minor and did not lead to a visible improvement of model diagnostics. The parameter estimates of the final model for tildrakizumab is summarized in Table 78.

L1508: ## **Post hoc Parameter Estimation:**
L1509: In addition to the NONMEM-reported parameter estimates, geometric means and %CV were calculated based on posthoc parameters of the final run for CL, V, KA and derived PK parameters such as the absorption and elimination half-life. The result is summarized in Table 79.
L1510: Subjects with psoriasis were characterized by a geometric mean (%CV) clearance of tildrakizumab of 0.32 L/day (38%), volume of distribution of 10.8 L (24%), absorption and elimination half-life (t_{1/2}) of 1.5 days (18%) and 23.4 days (23%), respectively.

L1514: ## **Covariate Analysis:**
L1515: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L1516: The impact of covariates is presented in
L1517: ## BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - BLA 761067
L1518: ## ILUMYA (tildrakizumab) injection, for subcutaneous use
L1519: Figure 22. All covariate effects except for BW were found to be small to modest. Heavy subjects had lower exposure. Extremes of weight (range: 40.6-222.2 kg) positively correlated to a -53% to +163% change in clearance and to a -43% to +107% change in volume compared to a subject with median weight. Extremes of serum albumin (range: 31-55 g/L) negatively correlated to a +36% to a -15% change in clearance compared to a subject with median albumin levels. Asians had 19% higher clearance than Whites. Alternatively, Japanese had a 17% greater clearance than Whites. Female subjects had a 10% higher clearance and a 7% higher volume than male subjects. Healthy subjects had 31% higher bioavailability than subjects with psoriasis. The lyophilized formulation had 5% lower bioavailability than the pre-filled syringe.
L1520: **Figure 22: Impact of Covariate on Tildrakizumab AUCss (100 mg Dose Administered Every 12 Weeks)**
L1521: The applicant's final population PK model for tildrakizumab is reasonable. The model adequately characterized the PK profile of tildrakizumab as indicated in the goodness-of-fit plots and the Visual Predictive Check plots. The inter-individual variability expressed as CV% was modest for clearance (38.2%) and the volume compartment (23.8%) suggesting that key PK parameters were reasonably estimated from available data. The population PK analysis was verified by the viewer and is deemed acceptable.
L1522: ## 13.5.5. Exposure-response analysis
L1523: The applicant submitted a separate exposure-response analysis report for tildrakizumab entitled *Population Pharmacokinetic/Pharmacodynamic and Exposure-Response Analysis of MK-3222 in Psoriasis Patients*.
L1524: ## Objectives:
L1525: The primary objectives of the E-R and longitudinal PK-PD model analyses were to:
L1526: - Quantify E-R for Week 12 PASI endpoints
- Understand the impact of key subject characteristics, especially weight, on the E-R relationship
- Compare projected PASI endpoints for 100 and 200 mg fixed-dose regimens relative to alternate weight-based regimens
- Characterize the longitudinal population PK-PD of PASI reduction for SC tildrakizumab in subjects with psoriasis
- Project the PASI response (PASI 75, PASI 90 and PASI 100 responders) over time for proposed tildrakizumab regimens in the induction and maintenance phases
L1527: ## Data:
L1528: Subjects from one Phase 2 trial (P05495) and two Phase 3 trials (P010 and P011) were included in the E-R and PK-PD modeling analyses. All subjects treated with tildrakizumab or placebo with valid post-treatment PASI results were included in the E-R analysis. Subjects treated with etanercept in Trial P011 were excluded in the modeling analysis. The average concentration from Week 1 to Week 12 was calculated using the individual PK parameters obtained from the final population pharmacokinetic (POP PK) model. Week 12 PASI 75, PASI 90 and PASI 100 response were imputed, if necessary, using last-observation-carried forward (LOCF).
L1529: The longitudinal PK-PD model analysis included the same subjects used in the E-R analyses. Time-course PASI results were collected up to Week 72. Time-matched tildrakizumab concentration were calculated using the individual PK parameters and the actual administered dose.
L1530: The baseline of the dataset for the PK-PD and E-R analysis is summarized in Table 80.
L1531: ## BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation - BLA 761067
## ILUMYA (tildrakizumab) injection, for subcutaneous use

L1535: ## Methods:
L1536: ## Exposure-Response for Week 12 PASI Response:
L1537: A maximum drug effect (E_{max}) logistic-regression E-R model was used to describe Week 12 PASI response.
L1538: \logit(\text{Pri}) = \text{Placebo response} + E_{max} \times \text{Cavg}12,i / (\text{EC}50 + \text{Cavg}12,i)
L1539: Where Pri is the probability of the PASI response of subject i, Cavg12,i is the average concentration of the first 12 weeks of treatment of subject i and EC50 is the concentration achieving 50% of the E_{max}
L1540: ## *Longitudinal PK-PD*
L1541: An indirect-effect model with drug-suppression of plaque formation and placebo-induced healing rate (the rate at which the psoriatic skin lesions heal over time either due to placebo or active treatment) was utilized in the PK-PD model. The model is illustrated in Figure 23.
L1542: Figure 23: Longitudinal PK-PD Model Schematic Diagram
L1543: The mathematical description of the model is given as:
L1544: \frac{d(\text{PTR}1_i)}{dt} = \text{kin}_i \times (1 - \text{Drug}_i) - \text{ktr}_i \times \text{PTR}1_i
L1545: \frac{d(\text{PTR}2_i)}{dt} = \text{ktr}_i \times \text{PTR}1_i - \text{ktr}_i \times \text{PTR}2_i
L1546: \frac{d(\text{PTR}3_i)}{dt} = \text{ktr}_i \times \text{PTR}2_i - \text{ktr}_i \times \text{PTR}3_i
L1547: \frac{d(\text{PASI}_i)}{dt} = \text{ktr}_i \times \text{PTR}3_i - \text{kout}_i \times \text{PASI}_i \times (1 + \text{Placebo}_i)
L1548: \text{Drug}_i = \text{Emax} \times \frac{\text{Cp}_i(t)}{(\text{Cp}_i(t) + \text{EC}50_i)}
L1549: \text{Placebo}_i = \text{Pmax}_i
L1550: Where i denotes subject and \text{P}_{\text{max}} is the maximum placebo response. The plaque formation rate is represented by the zero-order constant for production of the response (\text{kin}). PASI is the fraction of baseline, which ranges from 0 to 1. The plaque degradation rate is represented by the first-order rate constant for loss of the response (\text{kout}). The variables \text{PTR}1, \text{PTR}2, and \text{PTR}3 reference three sequential PASI transit compartments, each with transit rate constant (\text{ktr}). The drug effect is a standard Emax model, where \text{Cp}_{ij} is the predicted time course from the POP PK model using posthoc parameters. The placebo effect is a sudden increase in healing, which persists throughout the trial. The residual error model was additive in the logit-transformed PASI reduction.
L1551: ## Covariates
L1552: The impact of covariates, including weight, age, sex, race, region (Japan/non-Japan), psoriatic arthritis, prior biologic use, baseline PASI and trial, on the E-R model was tested using a forward addition (P &lt; 0.01) and backward elimination (P &lt; 0.001) approach. Covariates found to be significant in the E-R analysis were also tested in the PK-PD analysis.
L1553: ## Simulations
L1554: Simulations were used to characterize PASI response based on population parameters from the PK-PD/E-R models, including sampled covariates from the Phase 2 and 3 subject populations. This approach incorporated PK-PD/E-R model variability and parameter uncertainty. Simulations of Week 12 PASI response were performed by simulating 10000 trials with 10000
L1555: subjects per arm. The PK-PD simulations utilized 400 subjects per arm and 100 trial replications.
L1556: The E-R models were developed in R version 3.2.3 and the longitudinal PK-PD model was developed in non-linear mixed effects software (NONMEM) version 7.2. Simulations were performed in R.
L1557: ## Results:
L1558: ## *Exposure Efficacy Models*
L1559: The E_{max} model parameters for Week-12 PASI response are summarized in Table 81. Maximum drug effects were estimated as 62.2% for PASI 75, 37.9% for PASI 90 and 14.6% for PASI 100. The EC50 estimates were 0.36 µg/mL for PASI 75, 0.46 µg/mL for PASI 90 and 0.55 µg/mL for PASI 100. Subjects with greater weight had less placebo response for PASI 75, PASI 90 and PASI 100. Also, subjects with greater baseline PASI score and older subjects had less PASI 100 placebo response. The E-R relationship is illustrated in Figure 2.

L1563: ## *Longitudinal PK-PD Model*
L1564: The PK-PD approach provides an evaluation of the overall profile of tildrakizumab that can be used to make inferences for dose recommendations. Table 82 summarizes the parameters of the PK-PD model. Maximum placebo response was estimated as 19%, and maximum drug effect was set to 100% PASI reduction from baseline. The EC50 was 0.25 µg/mL, which is similar to the EC50s from the exposure-efficacy modeling.

L1576: ## PK-PD Model Simulations
L1577: Table 85 summarizes longitudinal PK-PD model simulations for the 100-mg and 200-mg doses and the effects of dose-titration in 100-mg partial responders. Compared to 100 mg, the 200-mg regimen increased PASI 75 by 7% and PASI 90 by 10% at Week 52 for the overall population. Titrating partial responders from 100 to 200 mg at 28 weeks had limited impact; although 15% more partial responders became PASI 75 responders, this increased the overall response rate by 2% because partial responders constituted a small portion of the overall population.

L1581: ## Exposure-Response Analysis for safety
L1582: The relationship of safety endpoints and the average exposure was explored in Part 1 (Weeks 1 to 12) of the two Phase 3 trials (P010 and P011). The AE event rates were tabulated by quartiles of the Cavg12 for each dose group (Table 86).
L1583: There was no trend in 0-12 AEs across exposure quartiles going from the lowest exposure quartile of the 100-mg group to the highest exposure quartile of the 200-mg group. Subjects at the highest exposure quartile of 200 mg had AE rates similar to those subjects in the lowest exposure quartile of the 100-mg regimen.

L1587: ## Integrated Risk-Benefit Analysis
L1588: Figure 24 shows the risk of Part 1&amp;2 infections/infestations relative to the PASI75 responder rate at Week28 for 100-mg or 200-mg doses in different weight subgroups. Collectively, the two regimens had similar risk-benefit profiles.
L1589: **Figure 24: Risk-Benefit Analysis for the Part 1&amp;2 Adverse Events (Week 0-28) and PASI 75 Response (Week 28)**
L1590: (Source of data: Figure 2.7.2, Summary of Clinical Pharmacology; Figure S3 on page 22 of Applicant's PK-PD report 04kx06.pdf)
L1591: The E_{max} model for Week 12 PASI response appears reasonable. The treatment response has reached plateau at dose of 100 mg. Additional dose such as 200 mg only increase efficacy marginally. Subjects with greater weight had lower response for PASI 75, PASI 90 and PASI 100. Also, subjects with greater baseline PASI score and older subjects had less PASI 100 response at Week 12.
L1592: E-R analysis did not reveal significant relationship for Week 12 PASI 75 response and AEs. Treatment with 200 mg for heavy subjects did not show significant benefits. Therefore, the 200-mg dose is not recommended in the label.
L1593: ## **14 Office Director (ODE III)**
L1594: I concur with the recommendation of the Division of Dermatology and Dental Products to approve ILUMYA (tildrakizumab) injection for the treatment of adults with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy. The recommended dose is 100 mg, administered subcutaneously. Data submitted in the BLA support the conclusion that the benefits of treatment with tildrakizumab in the intended population outweigh the identified risks. Product labeling and a Medication Guide will adequately communicate these benefits and risks to healthcare providers and patients, respectively. A REMS will not be required. Post-marketing required studies will assess: 1) maternal, fetal and infant risks of women exposed to tildrakizumab during pregnancy, 2) the long-term safety of tildrakizumab, and 3) the pharmacokinetics, safety and efficacy of tildrakizumab in pediatric subjects with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis ages 6 to less than 18 years.
L1595: Julie Beitz, MD

L1616: Submission type: Original BLA
L1617: Submission date: March 23, 2017
L1618: Drug: Tildrakizumab-asmn
L1619: Indication: For the treatment of adults with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy
L1620: Route: Subcutaneous injection
L1621: Applicant: Merck Sharp &amp; Dohme Corp.
L1622: ## **Background**
L1623: Merck Sharp &amp; Dohme Corp. submitted a Biologics License Application (BLA) dated March 23, 2017 under section 351(a) of the Public Health Service Act for ILUMYA (tildrakizumab-asmn) injection, for subcutaneous use. ILUMYA is an interleukin-23 antagonist indicated for the treatment of adults with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy. The dosing regimen is 100 mg administered by subcutaneous injection at Week 0, Week 4 and every 12 weeks thereafter.
L1624: The applicant submitted data from two adequate and well-controlled trials [Trial P010 and Trial P011], which provided evidence of the effectiveness of tildrakizumab for the treatment of adults with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy. In addition, the applicant conducted a comprehensive assessment of the safety of tildrakizumab in the target population. The size of the safety database and the safety evaluations were sufficient to characterize the local and systemic treatment-emergent adverse reactions. The applicant established the benefit of tildrakizumab for the proposed indication. Approval is supported by the data included in this submission.
L1625: ## **Recommendation:**
L1626: The clinical team recommends approval of ILUMYA (tildrakizumab-asmn) injection, for the treatment of adults with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy.
L1627: The clinical review is complete and has been added to the Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation, which will be uploaded to DARRTS when it is finalized. Refer to the Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation for the details.

L1720: ## 2. Comments to the Applicant
L1721: There are no comments or information request to be conveyed to the Applicant at this time.
L1722: ## 3. Main clinical pharmacology findings on *initial* review of the submission
L1723: Tildrakizumab (also known as MK-3222 and SCH 900222) is a humanized immunoglobulin G1/kappa (IgG1/\kappa) monoclonal antibody that binds to the p19 subunit of IL-23.
L1724: ## Proposed indication
L1725: - For the treatment of moderate to severe plaque psoriasis in adult patients who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy
L1726: ## Proposed dosing regimens
L1727: - The proposed dosing regimen is 100 mg tildrakizumab administered by subcutaneous injection at Weeks 0, 4, and every 12 weeks thereafter. (b) (4)
L1728: (b) (4)
L1729: Note: The doses used in the Phase 3 clinical development program were 100 mg and 200 mg tildrakizumab administered by subcutaneous injection at Weeks 0 and 4 followed by every 12 weeks (Q12W) dosing.
L1730: ## Proposed dosage form/presentation
L1731: - Single use prefilled syringe (PFS): 100 mg of tildrakizumab in 1.0 mL solution (100 mg/mL)
L1732: ## 3.1. Overview of clinical trials and clinical pharmacology program
L1733: The tildrakizumab psoriasis development program included 8 clinical studies in support of the proposed indication:
L1734: - Five Phase 1 studies in healthy subjects and subjects with psoriasis:Study P05661: single IV dose PK trial in healthy subjects
- Study P05776: single SC dose PK trial in healthy subjects
- Study P06306: intrinsic factor PK trial in healthy Japanese, Chinese, and Caucasian subjects
- Study P05382: multiple IV doses PK trial in subjects with psoriasis
- Study P009: relative BA between PFS and AI and effect of tildrakizumab on PK of CYP450 isoenzymes in subjects with psoriasis
- One Phase 2 dose ranging study in subjects with psoriasis:Study P05495
- Two Phase 3 efficacy/safety pivotal trials in subjects with psoriasis:Study P010
- Study P011
L1735: The Applicant also submitted study results from a Phase 1 clinical trial conducted in subjects Crohn's disease (Study P05839) to support the BLA.
L1736: See *Synopsis of Individual Studies* in Module 2.7.6 and *Tabular Listing of all Clinical Studies* in Module 5.2 for additional information.
L1737: See *Appendix 1* for the detailed summary of study design, tildrakizumab doses, and clinical pharmacology evaluations in these clinical trials.
L1738: ## 3.2. Biopharmaceutics
L1739: Tildrakizumab drug substance (DS) is expressed by Chinese Hamster Ovary (CHO) cell line. There had been two tildrakizumab DS manufacturing processes (Process 1 and Process 2) throughout different clinical development stages. Process 1 was the initial tildrakizumab DS manufacturing process which was used to supply Phase 1/2 clinical trials. Process 2 was used to supply Phase 3 clinical trials and is the commercial DS manufacturing process. Manufacturing changes from Process 1 to Process 2 included a manufacturing scale change in order to meet increased supply demands during clinical development. The Applicant conducted analytical comparability evaluations to support manufacturing changes of the DS used in clinical trials.
L1740: Three different tildrakizumab drug products including lyophilized powder (LYO), 1.0 mL PFS, and 1.0 mL autoinjector (AI) have been used across the clinical trials (Table 3.2). The two pivotal Phase 3 studies
L1741: used the 1.0 mL PFS presentation which is also the to-be-marketed product proposed for registration in the BLA. The 1.0 mL AI presentation was only used in Phase 1 Study P009. Study P009 explored the relative BA between the PFS and AI presentations in a limited number of subjects with psoriasis (N=10 for each presentation). The Applicant did not propose to register the AI presentation in the BLA.
L1742: **Table 3.2.** Tildrakizumab products with different drug substances, formulations, and/or presentations used in clinical trials. aStudy P009 evaluated the relative BA between the PFS and AI presentations in a limited number of subjects (N=10 for each presentation). PFS, prefilled syringe; AI, autoinjector
L1743: DS
Products	Process 1

L1744: **Reviewer's comments:** The biopharmaceutics program of the BLA is acceptable to support filing of the BLA. Because the commercial DS and the to-be-marketed drug product 1.0 mL PFS were used in pivotal Phase 3 trials, the BLA does not contain a PK comparability study to compare the to-be-marketed product to that used in the pivotal clinical trials.
L1745: The Applicant did not conduct clinical trials comparing PK between LYO used in Phase 1/2 clinical trials and PFS used in Phase 3 trials. The Applicant's population PK analysis (PK M&amp;S Analysis Report 04K8Q6) showed that LYO had 5% lower bioavailability than PFS. (b) (4)
L1746: (b) (4)
L1747: We defer the determination of the adequacy of the analytical comparability data to support the DS manufacturing changes to the OPQ review team.
L1748: ## 3.3. Pharmacokinetics and associated analytical methods
L1749: The Applicant characterized PK of tildrakizumab in healthy subjects and subjects with psoriasis in five Phase 1 trials with intensive PK sampling and in three Phase 2 and 3 trials with sparse PK sampling (see Appendix 1). The PK parameters and observed serum tildrakizumab concentrations were described in individual Clinical Study Reports located in Module 5.3.3 and Module 5.3.5.
L1750: The Applicant also conducted population PK analysis to support the characterization of tildrakizumab PK. The population PK analysis included covariate analyses to evaluate the effect of intrinsic and extrinsic factors. The population PK report (04K8Q6.pdf), the associated datasets, and analysis programs are found in Module 5.3.5.3.
L1751: ## Analytical methods for PK measurements
L1752: Tildrakizumab concentrations in human serum were quantified using a validated electrochemiluminescent (ECL) bridging assay. The PK assay validation reports (00M8G7.pdf and 03T5F4.pdf) are found in Module 5.3.1.4.
L1753: ## 3.4. Dose selection and exposure-response (E-R) relationship
L1754: In addition to the Phase 2 dose ranging study results, the Applicant conducted integrated E-R (population PK/PD) analyses for efficacy and safety using combined data from the Phase 2 study (P05495) and Phase 3 studies (P010 and P011) to justify the proposed dosing regimen. The summary of the dose section rationale, E-R for efficacy, E-R for safety are found in *Clinical Overview (Section 3)* and *Summary of Clinical Pharmacology (Sections 1.5 and 2.6)*. The exposure-response analysis report (04KX06.pdf), the associated datasets, and analysis programs are found in Module 5.3.5.3.
L1755: ## 3.5. Immunogenicity
L1756: The summaries of the immunogenicity results were provided in *Clinical Overview (Section 3.6.1)* and *Summary of Clinical Pharmacology (Section 4.1)*. The Applicant also submitted an *Immunogenicity Summary Report (04FCNM.pdf)* in Module 5.3.5.3 that provided the detailed information of immunogenicity assessment methods and results. Immunogenicity incidences and their clinical impacts on PK, efficacy and safety were assessed and reported in the BLA.
L1757: The serum anti-drug antibodies (ADA) were detected using an ECL assay on the Meso Scale Discovery (MSD) platform. Confirmed ADA positive samples were tested for neutralizing ADA (NAb). NAb was detected using a competitive ligand binding assay. The immunogenicity assay validation reports are located in Module 5.3.1.4. The sensitivity and drug tolerance level of the ADA and NAb immunogenicity assays used in Phase 3 trials are summarized in Table 3.5.
L1758: **Table 3.5. L1760: **Reviewer's comments on drug tolerance of the ADA assays:** For the detection of 500 ng/mL ADA positive control, the ECL binding ADA assay had a drug tolerance level of [REDACTED] (b) (4) of tildrakizumab in serum and the NAb assay had a drug tolerance level of [REDACTED] (b) (4) of tildrakizumab in serum. In psoriasis Phase 3 trials, the mean\pmSD serum tildrakizumab concentrations following the 200 mg and 100 mg dosing regimens are presented in Figure 3.5. As a reference point, the mean\pmSD steady state trough concentrations at Week 16 were 2.25\pm 1.88 mcg/mL and 1.22\pm 0.939 for the 200 mg and 100 mg doses, respectively, in Study P010; and 2.99\pm 2.16 mcg/mL and 1.47\pm 1.12 for the 200 mg and 100 mg doses, respectively, in Study P011. The initial assessment of the PK data does not indicate that drug interference would be a major issue for the ADA assay to detect ADA &gt;500 ng/mL because the mean\pmSD steady state serum tildrakizumab levels appear to be generally below the drug tolerance level of [REDACTED] (b) (4) of the ADA assay. In contrast, the detection of NAb could be interfered with the presence of tildrakizumab in serum because the mean\pmSD steady state serum tildrakizumab concentrations were above the drug tolerance level of [REDACTED] (b) (4) of the NAb assay. The initial assessment indicates that drug interference to the NAb assay presents a review issue for the Clinical Pharmacology and OBP review teams.
L1761: ## 3.6. Drug-drug interactions (DDI)
L1762: The Applicant conducted a clinical DDI study (Study P009) that evaluated the effect of tildrakizumab on the PK of five CYP450 probe substrates (midazolam, omeprazole, caffeine, dextromethorphan, and warfarin+vitamin K) in subjects with psoriasis. Clinical Study Report for Study P009 was located in Module 5.3.3.4.
L1763: ## 3.7. Proposal for a pediatric program
L1764: No clinical studies have been conducted to evaluate tildrakizumab in pediatric patients with psoriasis. According to the Agreed Initial Pediatric Stud Plan (Agreed iPS), the Applicant has requested (1) a waiver of studies for evaluation of tildrakizumab in pediatric patients with psoriasis from 0 to &lt;6 years of age, and (2) a deferral of studies for evaluation of tildrakizumab in pediatric patients with psoriasis from 6 to &lt;18 years of age. Module 1.9.4 of the BLA contains the Agreed iPS.

L1817: ## CONSULTATIVE REVIEW AND EVALUATION OF CLINICAL DATACONSULT #11682
L1818: Consultant Reviewer: Jean Kim MD, MA, Medical Officer, ODE1-DPP
Consultation Requestor: Dawn Williams BSN, RPM, ODEIII-DDDP
Subject of Request: BLA 761067: Tildrakizumab
Date of Request: 5/3/2017
Date Received: 8/29/2017
Desired Completion Date: 10/23/2017
L1819: ## I. Executive Summary
L1820: The Sponsor (Sun Pharma) submitted a BLA currently under review by the Division of Dermatology and Dental Products (DDDP) for tildrakizumab for the indication of psoriasis on 3/24/2017. Efficacy was evaluated in two Phase 3, randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind trials (P010 and P011). Placebo-controlled data from the first 12 weeks of the two Phase 3 trials was pooled with placebo-controlled data from the first 16 weeks of the Phase 2 study, P003. (The Phase 3 trials also each had a 192-week long-term safety extension phase.)
L1821: Psychiatric adverse events (AEs), including three suicidal ideation and behavior (SI/B) events, were identified by DDDP from the Sponsor's initial submission; DDDP is requesting that the Division of Psychiatry Products (DPP) review the psychiatric AE study data in the tildrakizumab trials and the requested results of a retrospective Columbia Classification and Assessment of Suicidal Adverse Events (C-CASA) tabulation analysis to provide input on whether there are any concerns about a psychiatric or SI/B AE signal, and if so, what follow-up and labeling guidance should be provided.
L1822: Overall, the rates of psychiatric AEs found in the studies during the placebo-controlled phase was around 2% on study drug (versus 1% placebo), and did not demonstrate nominally statistically significant differences between the study drug population and placebo. The retrospective C-CASA analysis did not uncover any additional pertinent SI/B events during the placebo-controlled period. During the Safety Update reporting period, two suicides did occur during the Phase 3 extension studies, but these did not appear directly related to the study drug. I conclude that based on the data provided, there is no clear association between tildrakizumab and SI/B or other psychiatric conditions.
L1823: However, there remain some documented possible effects with other interleukin (IL) inhibitors and psychiatric AEs, as well as increased baseline psychiatric risk in subjects with psoriasis, that warrant ongoing monitoring and caution. The two extension phase suicides (and those in other psoriasis drug trials) point to this issue of baseline psychiatric risk, even apart from drug-related concerns. Although psychiatric safety monitoring has limitations and will not detect or prevent many psychiatric AEs or even most suicides, it is still a reasonable, cost-efficient, and relatively simple way to potentially address some events as they develop and trigger additional
L1824: psychiatric attention as warranted. I strongly recommend that in future protocols for patients with psoriasis, or those who at least have a psychiatric history (including psychotropic medication use), *prospective* psychiatric safety monitoring be performed during clinical trials, including the extension phase, at each visit.
L1825: ## II. Background
L1826: Tildrakizumab is a novel high affinity humanized immunoglobulin G1/kappa antibody that specifically binds to the IL-23p19 subunit and blocks the interaction of human IL-23 with the IL-23 receptor. Tildrakizumab, in a 100 mg/mL solution, is administered as a subcutaneous injection via 1 mL single-dose prefilled glass syringe for the treatment of adults with moderate-to-severe plaque psoriasis who are candidates for systemic therapy or phototherapy.
L1827: Other psoriasis systemic therapies have targeted receptors such as IL-12, IL-23, IL-17, and Tumor Necrosis Factor (TNF)-\alpha. Some of these receptors have been associated with downstream psychiatric effects and are theorized to modulate neurotransmission.
L1828: IL-23 is a cytokine, produced mainly by activated myeloid cells, signaled by a heterodimeric IL-23 receptor complex combining an IL-23 receptor and IL-12R\beta1. IL-23 is known to drive autoimmune reactions in mouse models and human diseases via activation of a subset of T cells (Th17). Tildrakizumab is postulated to improve psoriasis via selective IL-23 blockade, and may leave IL-12 activity largely unaffected.
L1829: Increasing evidence suggests that cytokines are key mediators in the pathophysiology of major depressive disorder (possibly via hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis effects) and other psychiatric disorders like schizophrenia as well. Research studies have also noted altered cytokine levels (particularly IL-6 elevations in one review1 and IL-2 and IL-4 and Transforming Growth Factor (TGF)-\beta in another meta-analysis2) in the cerebrospinal fluid of suicide attempters and completers compared to non-suicidal subjects. Psoriasis patients are also well-known to have higher rates of depression, although it is unclear if the rates are mainly due to a primary immune-mediated cause, the high psychosocial burden of the illness, or a combination of both factors contributing to that increased risk. Adverse psychiatric effects with other IL inhibitors and anti-TNF-\alpha agents used to treat psoriasis remain an area of concern. These agents include brodalumab (IL-17A inhibitor), infliximab (TNF-\alpha inhibitor) and apremilast (PDE-4 inhibitor, which has downstream effects on cytokine blockade including IL-23 and IL-12), which have raised questions of potential disinhibition, anxiety, mood changes and/or lability, or suicidality. (Complicating matters is that some anti-psoriatic drugs like etanercept and adalimumab that are TNF-\alpha blockers may also have antidepressant activity.)
L1830: 1 Gananca L et al. The role of cytokines in the pathophysiology of suicidal behavior. *Psychoneuroendocrinology* 63(2016): 296-310.
L1831: 2 Ducasse D et al. A meta-analysis of cytokines in suicidal behavior. *Brain, Behavior, and Immunity*. 46(2015): 203-211.
L1832: A literature review shows no known clear associations between IL-23 and psychiatric symptoms, although some researchers postulate that IL-23 has such effects and shows altered levels in schizophrenia3. One recent study showed no changes in IL-23 plasma levels after antidepressant treatment in patients with MDD4, but another showed possible increased serum IL-23 levels in patients with schizophrenia who were on depot antipsychotic medication versus healthy controls5. The clinical implications of these studies remain unclear.
L1833: ## III. Review of Clinical Data
L1834: ## A. Selection of Relevant Clinical Trials

L1838: (From Mid-Cycle Meeting Agenda Summary, Kevin Clark MD and Melinda McCord MD)
L1839: As noted above, one Phase 2 (P003, also referred to as P05495) and two Phase 3 clinical trials (P010 and P011) were performed with tildrakizumab. A total of 1994 subjects with moderate to severe plaque psoriasis were exposed to tildrakizumab during these Phase 2/3 trials in all phases (1413 during the placebo-controlled phase). The two Phase 3 trials used tildrakizumab doses of 100 mg and 200 mg and had a placebo-controlled phase only up until Week 12, after which subjects were re-randomized and continued dosing until Week 28. (Study P011 also had an active comparator arm using etanercept.) The Phase 2 trial had four different dose arms (5 mg, 25 mg, 100 mg, and 200 mg) and a placebo arm until Week 16 (before dose adjustment and continued dosing until Week 52). For adverse event analysis, these trials were pooled during
L1840: 3 Debnath M and Berk M. Functional implications of the IL-23/IL-17 immune axis in schizophrenia. *Molecular Neurobiology*. Nov 2016: Epub ahead of print.
L1841: 4 Kim J, et al. Plasma levels of IL-23 and IL-17 before and after antidepressant treatment in patients with major depressive disorder. *Psychiatry Investigation* 10(2013): 294-299.
L1842: 5 O'Connell KE, et al. Increased IL-23 levels in schizophrenia patients treated with depot antipsychotic medication. *Cytokine*. 73(2015): 196-8.
L1843: the placebo-controlled phases. (Both Phase 3 trials also had an optional long-term safety extension phase up to 192 weeks, with 621 subjects on 100 mg and 616 subjects on 200 mg.)
L1844: No completed suicides occurred during these trials during the Phase 2 and 3 study base period (prior to the extension phase). The Sponsor identified three possible SI/B events (all on tildrakizumab) in the entire program, including one from Phase 1 and two from Phase 3 (one of which was during the extension phase), according to the original submission.
L1845: The Sponsor then conducted an additional Retrospective Columbia Classification Algorithm of Suicide Assessment (C-CASA) adjudication and submitted the report on 10/3/2017. The analysis noted one additional SI/B case during Phase 3 not previously counted: one subject with two suicidal ideation events during a single hospitalization (counted as one SI/B event; this event was not noted in the original submission, although AEs of worsening of depression and bipolar disorder for the same hospitalization were initially reported). They also noted three additional SI/B cases in the updated extension period, including two suicides and one suicidal ideation event.
L1846: ## B. Psychiatric Inclusion/Exclusion Criteria
L1847: P003: No psychiatric exclusion criteria specified.
L1848: P010: No psychiatric exclusion criteria specified.
L1849: P011: Excluded subjects with a history of alcohol or drug abuse in the past year.
L1850: ## C. Psychiatric Safety Monitoring
L1851: None of the Phase 2 and 3 studies used specific psychiatric or suicidality scales for safety monitoring or indicated special follow-up procedures in adverse event monitoring protocols. A couple of Quality of Life scales were used in the studies, but none had specific psychiatric symptom questions.
L1852: P003: Assessments at Screening, Baseline (up to 28 days post-screening), Day 14, 28, 42, 56, 84, 112 and Follow-Up. AE monitoring at all visits. No specific psychiatric scales. (Only Quality of Life scales: Health Assessment Questionnaire, which is a PRO noting degrees of pain and disability, and Dermatology Life Quality Index (DLQI), both assessed at Baseline and at Day 84, 112, and Follow-up.)
L1853: P010: Assessments at Screening, Baseline (up to Day 28), monthly intervals. DLQI and HAQ at Baseline, Week 12, 28, 40, 52, 64.
L1854: P011: Same as P010 except only DLQI not HAQ.
L1855: This lack of any psychiatric safety monitoring in these clinical trials is concerning, given the increasing evidence of psychiatric morbidity in patients with psoriasis, and given that suicides have occurred in other psoriasis trials.
L1856: ## D. Coding of Psychiatric Adverse Events
L1857: The Sponsor used MedDRA version 19.1 to code AE terms to preferred terms in the Phase 2 and 3 studies. I reviewed all the ADAE.xpt datasets for coding accuracy between AETERM and AEDECOD and found them satisfactory from a psychiatric AE perspective. I also reviewed subjects under the Injury, Poisoning, and Procedural Complications SOC under AEBODSYS, and no obvious miscodings of hidden psychiatric events were evident; they were all likely accidental/non-psychiatric in nature based on the limited information provided, except for the events that were coded already as psychiatric.
L1858: Psychiatric AE terms were found under AEBODSYS category for Psychiatric Disorder SOC. Under AEDECOD (preferred terms) they were as follows:
L1859: P003—insomnia, adjustment disorder with depressed mood, stress, nervousness, mood swings, depression
L1860: P010—insomnia, anxiety, depression, irritability, agitation, mood swings, libido decreased, panic attack, suicide attempt, adjustment disorder
L1861: P011—depression, major depression, insomnia, emotional disorder, anxiety, irritability, sleep disorder
L1862: There were also numerous AEs coded under fatigue or tiredness, but I decided not to include them as psychiatric events since it would be difficult to distinguish their etiology from both the underlying medical condition of psoriasis and possible systemic drug effects versus any depressive etiology. Fatigue/tiredness AEs did not occur on drug at rates higher than placebo.
L1863: **Table 2 Rates of Fatigue/Tiredness AEs in Phase 2 and 3 Tildrakizumab Studies (Placebo-Controlled Phase)**
L1864: Arm	AEs	Crude Incidence Rate
Etanercept 50 mg	4	4/313=1.2%
Tildrakizumab 100 mg	18	4/705=0.6%
Tildrakizumab 200 mg	7	7/708=1.0%
Placebo	5	5/357=1.4%

L1882: There were no nominally significant differences between tildrakizumab versus placebo for the above psychiatric AEs or subcategories per Fisher's exact test, even if via crude rates psychiatric AEs occurred at a higher rate on drug (2%) versus placebo (1%). However, power is limited by sample size, and exposure durations varied slightly with the Phase 2 versus Phase 3 trials.
L1883: ## ii. Suicidal Ideation and Behavior (SI/B)
L1884: No SI/B-related deaths were reported in any of these three studies during the base study period. There were two suicides as per the October 3, 2017 Safety Update Report that occurred during the extension phase (one subject originally from P010 and one from P011).
L1885: There was also one subject in P010 (b) (6) who died reportedly from a toxic reaction between fluoxetine and cyclobenzaprine (although it is unclear how long the subject had been on those medications, or if elevated doses were taken—the autopsy report did note “prescription drug abuse”) during the extension phase. The subject had a past history of depression and suicidal ideation. (There were also two deaths secondary to alcohol abuse-related medical sequelae during the Phase 2 and 3 trials, one of whom was adjudicated as “unknown cause” during C-CASA analysis.)
L1886: There was one suicide attempt during the placebo-controlled phase in Study P010, and two suicidal ideation events during the extension phase in Study P011. (There was also one suicidal ideation event in Phase 1 Study 007.) All of these events occurred on tildrakizumab.
L1887: The differences between drug and placebo for the 12 to 16 week phase for SI/B events are not nominally statistically significant. Extension phase SI/B events are unlikely to be drug-related, given the length of time subjects have been exposed to drug before events occur without prior incident.

L1898: ## iv. Anxiety and Insomnia
L1899: No clear associations are evident between tildrakizumab and anxiety and insomnia AEs when looking at the placebo-controlled AE data. There is no clear evidence of activation-related phenomena with this drug based on reported AEs.
L1900: ## v. Concomitant Medications
L1901: Subjects in each treatment arm were taking similar percentages of at least one concomitant psychotropic medication (slightly less in the etanercept group). This seems to indicate similar rates of baseline psychiatric morbidity between treatment groups.
L1902: **Table 8 Phase 2 and 3 Subjects Taking Concomitant Psychotropic Medications at Screening**
L1903: Psychotropic Medications	Number of Subjects-Tildrakizumab Arms (100 and 200 mg) N=1413	Number of Subjects-Placebo Arms (N=357)	Number of Subjects-Etanercept (N=313)
"Psychoanaleptics," i.e. stimulants and antidepressants	135 (9.6%)	35 (9.8%)	27 (8.6%)
"Psycholeptics," i.e. benzodiazepines, sleep medications, antipsychotics	108 (7.6%)	28 (7.8%)	17 (5.4%)
L1904: 23 subjects with psychiatric AEs (out of 38 total, 61%) were taking concomitant psychotropic medications at some point during the study (17 during screening, or 45%): 6 (2 of them during screening) from Study P003, 11 (7 during screening) from P010, and 6 (4 during screening) from P011. All but one (on etanercept who was on it during screening) were on study drug as well. This data indicates that the population on study drug developing psychiatric AEs might have had more pre-existing psychiatric morbidity than those developing psychiatric AEs who were not on study drug.
L1905: Treatment groups also were taking similar amounts of concomitant steroid medications (which have the potential to adversely affect mood, sleep, and anxiety). Thus, it does not seem likely that steroid medications confounded the comparison of psychiatric event rates.

L1911: ## G. Scale Data
L1912: The quality-of-life scales used are not designed for the assessment of primary psychiatric symptomatology. No other psychiatric scales were used in these studies.
L1913: ## IV. Other Consults/History
L1914: Tildrakizumab (an IL-23 p19 subunit inhibitor) is one of a few approved and/or studied medications that also act on IL-23, such as guselkumab (a monoclonal antibody to the p19 subunit to IL-23, FDA-approved in July 2017), ustekinumab (a monoclonal antibody to the p40 subunit of IL-12 and IL-23, FDA-approved in September 2009) and [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L1915: [REDACTED] No clear SI/B or depression or anxiety signals were noted during the trials for these medications thus far.
L1916: Guselkumab had one completed suicide occur during the extension phase which was not felt to be related to the study medication, as per our division's prior consult review by John Umhau MD on 4/10/2017. No suicide attempts and one suicidal ideation AE on guselkumab during the placebo-controlled phase were noted in the trials. Both subjects with SI/B events had prior histories of depression. Retrospective C-CASA adjudication only noted an incidence rate of 0.10 per 100 subject-years for SI/B events for subjects on guselkumab. (Similar rates were found with secukinumab 0.06/100 and ixekizumab 0.14/100.) Overall psychiatric AEs on guselkumab also did not occur at a higher rate than placebo.
L1917: Other cytokine receptor inhibitors have been approved for the treatment of psoriasis, with concerns about effects on psychiatric symptoms and SI/B; in particular, brodalumab, which is an IL-17a receptor antagonist, received a boxed warning due to six suicides that occurred during its clinical trial program. Apremilast is a PDE-4 inhibitor that has some downstream effects on IL-23 and had some possible associations with depression and anxiety (leading to inclusion in the label). However, overall, the current literature on IL-23 and association with any psychiatric effects remains extremely limited and unremarkable.
L1918: ## V. Conclusions and Recommendations
L1919: Overall, the rates of psychiatric AEs were low in the tildrakizumab treatment group in the Phase 2 and 3 studies reviewed above. The low rates might, in part, be due to lack of formal psychiatric symptom monitoring in these trials; however, this interpretation can be counterbalanced by the lack of any psychiatric exclusion criteria for these studies (aside from substance abuse exclusion in one trial) and baseline elevated rates of depression and suicide risk in subjects with psoriasis. There was a slightly higher crude rate incidence of psychiatric AEs in the drug treatment group versus placebo (2% versus 1%) in the placebo-controlled phases of the Phase 2 and 3 trials, but these differences were not nominally statistically significant. SI/B events were extremely rare before C-CASA adjudication; only one confirmed suicide attempt occurred during the Phase 2 and 3 trials, in a subject with high psychiatric comorbidity (diagnosis of schizophrenia and on antipsychotic medication), although the timing of the incident (11 days after first dose) does not completely rule out a possible drug association.
L1920: Retrospective C-CASA adjudication only uncovered one additional suicidal ideation event (associated with a depression and bipolar AE previously reported) during the extension period and added one death of unknown cause (unlikely to be due to study medication, with concomitant alcohol abuse) which occurred in the placebo-controlled period.
L1921: During the update period up until 10/3/17, one additional suicidal ideation event and two suicides occurred during the extension studies. None of these new events appear to be associated with the study drug, and they all occurred in the context of pre-existing psychiatric morbidity.
L1922: For depression, anxiety, and insomnia events in these studies, drug causality seemed unlikely.
L1923: Other IL inhibitors have had potential psychiatric and CNS effects, and a class association cannot be completely ruled out at this time, although no clear signals are noted thus far and postmarketing data evaluation is ongoing.
L1924: My recommendations are as follows:
L1925: - Given the two suicides recently reported in the extension safety update, and other ones that have occurred in clinical trial programs in patients with psoriasis, if not already implemented, I strongly recommend that DDDP ask Sponsors that in future protocols for all patients with psoriasis, or at the very least those who have a reported psychiatric history (including medications) before or during the trial, prospective psychiatric safety monitoring during clinical trials, including the extension phase, occur at each visit. This monitoring should include suicidal ideation and behavior monitoring that maps to C-CASA categories (such as the Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS) or Sheehan Suicidality Tracking Scale (S-STS)), and at least one psychiatric symptom monitoring scale such as the Brief Psychiatric Rating Scale (BPRS) (which screens for a wide range of psychiatric conditions) and/or a depression scale such as the Patient Health Questionnaire (PHQ) or Physician Depression Questionnaire (PDQ). Anxiety or insomnia
L1926: scales can also be considered as well. (The HADS (Hospital Anxiety and Depression Scale) has been used in some psoriasis trials.)
L1927: This psychiatric safety monitoring remains prudent given the baseline increased SI/B and psychiatric risk with subjects with psoriasis, plus the added risk of unknown potential immune modulator drug effects on psychiatric symptoms. All screening tools for suicidality and depression remain limited in terms of ability to predict or prevent SI/B events. Evidence on screening-related outcomes also remains inconclusive and contradictory, although of interest, the Canadian Dermatology-Rheumatology Comorbidity Initiative came to a consensus recommendation of depression screening for patients with psoriasis in the clinical setting.6
L1928: Overall, this safety monitoring is a relatively simple and efficient method to potentially proactively intervene with at least some psychiatric concerns as they arise, and to provide better care and protection for clinical subjects in a population known to be at increased risk of psychiatric events. The data collected is also potentially helpful in terms of determining any existing direct drug-related effects or other causes or trends associated with SI/B or depression in this population.
L1929: - Informed Consent documents should also advise subjects to contact the investigator if they experience new or worsening depression or suicidal ideation, because the intervals between visits can be very wide in some extension studies.
- I do not recommend exclusion of subjects with psoriasis with a psychiatric history and/or medications from future trials, as it is important for them to be studied, in part because they represent a significant portion of real-world patients with psoriasis. For those subjects with a history of suicidality, the reviewer(s) and Sponsor(s) should usually consider that exclusion determination on a case-by-case risk-benefit basis for each protocol. As a general rule, those with recent suicidal behavior (i.e., attempts) within the past month and/or active SI/B on screening should be excluded and referred as needed to appropriate psychiatric intervention.
- I do not recommend specific psychiatric warning language in the tildrakizumab label at this time, as there is insufficient evidence of increased risk of SI/B or other psychiatric issues thus far with tildrakizumab, in terms of direct drug-related effect, based on the current clinical trial data available. There is also no preliminary research thus far showing any clear association with IL-23 and psychiatric sequelae.
L1930: 6 Roublie C, et al. Evidence-based recommendations for the management of comorbidities in rheumatoid arthritis, psoriasis, and psoriatic arthritis: expert opinion of the Canadian Dermatology-Rheumatology Comorbidity Initiative. *Journal of Rheumatology*. 42(10):1767-1780.

L1945: Figure 7 illustrates the multiplicity testing strategy for Trial P010, comparing tildrakizumab (MK-3222) at 200 mg and 100 mg against placebo at week 12, using a sequential gatekeeping approach.
The strategy is structured in three main comparisons, each involving two endpoints (PASI75 & PGA "clear" or "minimal" for the top row, PASI90 for the middle row, and PASI100 for the bottom row).
Top Row (PASI75 & PGA "clear" or "minimal"):
Comparison 1: tildrakizumab 200 mg vs placebo.
Comparison 2: tildrakizumab 100 mg vs placebo.
If both comparisons are significant (p < 0.05), the analysis proceeds to the middle row.
Middle Row (PASI90):
Comparison 3: tildrakizumab 200 mg vs placebo.
Comparison 4: tildrakizumab 100 mg vs placebo.
If both comparisons are significant (p < 0.05), the analysis proceeds to the bottom row.
Bottom Row (PASI100):
Comparison 5: tildrakizumab 200 mg vs placebo.
Comparison 6: tildrakizumab 100 mg vs placebo.
The flowchart shows that if the top row comparisons are significant, the middle row comparisons are tested. If the middle row comparisons are significant, the bottom row comparisons are tested.
L1946: Figure 8 is a flowchart illustrating the Multiplicity Testing Strategy for Trial P011, comparing Tildrakizumab (MK-3222) 200 mg (left column) against Tildrakizumab (MK-3222) 100 mg (right column).
The strategy involves multiple comparisons across different time points (Week 12 and Week 28) and endpoints (PASI75 & PGA "clear" or "minimal", PASI90, PASI100).
The flowchart structure is as follows:
Week 12 Comparisons (Top Row):Left: vs. placebo at week 12, PASI75 & PGA "clear" or "minimal".
Right: vs. placebo at week 12, PASI75 & PGA "clear" or "minimal".

Week 12 Comparisons (Second Row):Left: vs. placebo at week 12, PASI90.
Right: vs. placebo at week 12, PASI90.

Week 12 Comparisons (Third Row):Left: vs. etanercept at week 12, PASI75 & PGA "clear" or "minimal".
Right: vs. etanercept at week 12, PASI75 & PGA "clear" or "minimal".

Week 28 Comparisons (Fourth Row):Left: vs. etanercept at week 28, PASI75 & PGA "clear" or "minimal".
Right: vs. etanercept at week 28, PASI75 & PGA "clear" or "minimal".

Week 12 Comparisons (Fifth Row):Left: vs. placebo at week 12, PASI100.
Right: vs. placebo at week 12, PASI100.

Week 12 Comparisons (Sixth Row):Left: vs. etanercept at week 12, PASI90.
Right: vs. etanercept at week 12, PASI90.

Week 12 Comparisons (Bottom Row):Left: vs. etanercept at week 12, PASI100.
Right: vs. etanercept at week 12, PASI100.

Arrows indicate dependencies between comparisons, suggesting a hierarchical or sequential testing approach.

L1954: Figure 18 displays two graphs showing Mean tildrakizumab concentration (MK-3222 concentration in \mu\text{g/mL}) versus Time (weeks) for subjects in Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials, categorized by ADA status. The left graph is for Tildrakizumab 100 mg, and the right graph is for Tildrakizumab 200 mg. Both graphs show concentration profiles over 72 weeks, with dosing time points marked at 0, 4, 12, 16, 28, 40, 52, 64, and 72 weeks. The Y-axis is logarithmic, ranging from 0.01 to 10. The ADA categories and sample sizes (n) are listed in the legend for each graph. For Tildrakizumab 100 mg, categories include Inconclusive (n=8), Negative (n=342), Non-treatment emergent positive, NAb negative (n=21), Non-treatment emergent positive, NAb positive (n=3), Treatment emergent positive, NAb negative (n=16), and Treatment emergent positive, NAb positive (n=10). For Tildrakizumab 200 mg, categories include Inconclusive (n=25), Negative (n=299), Non-treatment emergent positive, NAb negative (n=21), Non-treatment emergent positive, NAb positive (n=4), Treatment emergent positive, NAb negative (n=19), and Treatment emergent positive, NAb positive (n=12). The graphs illustrate that subjects with ADA (especially treatment emergent positive, NAb positive) generally show lower tildrakizumab concentrations over time compared to ADA negative subjects.
L1955: Figure 19 displays four Goodness of Fit (GoF) plots for the Base Population PK Model, arranged in a 2x2 grid. The plots are labeled:
Top Left: DV vs. PRED (sdtabPK028)
Top Right: DV vs. IPRED (sdtabPK028)
Bottom Left: CWRES vs. PRED (sdtabPK028)
Bottom Right: CWRES vs. TAD (sdtabPK028)
All plots show observed data points (DV or CWRES) against predicted values (PRED or IPRED) or time (TAD). A red line represents the model fit, and dashed lines indicate the 95% confidence interval (CI) for the predicted values or the mean of the CWRES.
The DV vs. PRED plot shows DV (ng/mL) on the Y-axis (0 to 60000) versus PRED (ng/mL) on the X-axis (0 to 60000). The data points generally follow the red line, indicating a good fit.
The DV vs. IPRED plot shows DV (ng/mL) on the Y-axis (0 to 60000) versus IPRED (ng/mL) on the X-axis (0 to 60000). The data points generally follow the red line, indicating a good fit.
The CWRES vs. PRED plot shows CWRES (Y-axis, -5.0 to 7.5) versus PRED (ng/mL) (X-axis, 0 to 60000). The CWRES values are centered around 0, with the red line closely following the mean CWRES, indicating a good fit.
The CWRES vs. TAD plot shows CWRES (Y-axis, -5.0 to 7.5) versus TAD (days) (X-axis, 0 to 100). The CWRES values are centered around 0, with the red line closely following the mean CWRES, indicating a good fit.
L1956: Figure 20 displays four plots assessing the goodness of fit for the final population PK model for Tildrakizumab (sdtabPK038).
Top Left: DV vs. PRED (Observed vs. Predicted Concentration). The Y-axis is DV [ng/ml] (0 to 60000) and the X-axis is PRED [ng/ml] (0 to 60000). The data points generally follow the 1:1 line (red solid line) and the 95% prediction interval (black dashed lines), indicating a good fit.
Top Right: DV vs. IPRED (Observed vs. Individual Predicted Concentration). The Y-axis is DV [ng/ml] (0 to 60000) and the X-axis is IPRED [ng/ml] (0 to 60000). The data points generally follow the 1:1 line and the 95% prediction interval, indicating a good fit.
Bottom Left: CWRES vs. PRED (Conditional Weighted Residuals vs. Predicted Concentration). The Y-axis is CWRES (0.0 to 7.5) and the X-axis is PRED [ng/ml] (0 to 60000). The CWRES values are randomly scattered around the zero line (red solid line), with 95% prediction interval (black dashed lines) spanning approximately -5.0 to 5.0. This suggests no systematic bias.
Bottom Right: CWRES vs. TAD (Conditional Weighted Residuals vs. Time After Dose). The Y-axis is CWRES (0.0 to 7.5) and the X-axis is TAD [days] (0 to 100). The CWRES values are randomly scattered around the zero line, with 95% prediction interval (black dashed lines) spanning approximately -5.0 to 5.0. This suggests no systematic bias related to time after dose.

L1959: Schematic diagram of the Longitudinal PK-PD Model. The model shows drug concentration (Cp) entering a central compartment (Cp) via absorption (ka). Cp is eliminated via CL/V. Cp influences the production of PTR1 (PTR1 compartment) via a Drug effect (kin). PTR1 transitions sequentially to PTR2 (ktr) and PTR3 (ktr). PTR3 transitions to PASI (ktr). PASI is influenced by Placebo (kout) and leads to Healing. The compartments are Cp, PTR1, PTR2, PTR3, and PASI.

L1961: Figure 3.5 displays two line graphs showing Serum tildrakizumab concentrations (mcg/mL) over Time (Weeks) for Phase 3 studies P010 and P011, comparing 100 mg and 200 mg dosing regimens.
Study P010: The graph shows serum tildrakizumab concentrations peaking around Week 2 (approx. 6.5 mcg/mL for 200 mg, 4 mcg/mL for 100 mg) and then declining, stabilizing around 2-3 mcg/mL by Week 16. Both doses show similar steady-state trough concentrations.
Study P011: The graph shows serum tildrakizumab concentrations peaking around Week 2 (approx. 6 mcg/mL for 200 mg, 4 mcg/mL for 100 mg) and then declining, stabilizing around 2-3 mcg/mL by Week 16. Both doses show similar steady-state trough concentrations.
Figure 3.5. Serum tildrakizumab concentrations observed in Phase 3 studies. (Source of Data: CSR for Studies P010 and P011; Figure 2.7.25.7, Summary of Clinical Pharmacology)
